VFOG: Variance-Reduced Fast Optimistic Gradient Methods for a Class of Nonmonotone Generalized Equations
Quoc Tran-Dinh, Nghia Nguyen-Trung

Abstract
We develop a novel optimistic gradient-type algorithmic framework, combining both Nesterov’s acceleration and variance-reduction techniques, to solve a class of generalized equations involving possibly nonmonotone operators in data-driven applications. Our framework covers a wide class of stochastic variance-reduced schemes, including mini-batching, and control variate unbiased and biased estimators. We establish that our method achieves 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 / k 2 ) \mathcal{O}\big(1/k^{2}\big) convergence rates in expectation on the squared norm of residual under the Lipschitz continuity and a “co-hypomonotonicity-type” assumptions, improving upon non-accelerated counterparts by a factor of 1 / k 1/k . We also prove faster o ⁡ ( 1 / k 2 ) o\big(1/k^{2}\big) convergence rates, both in expectation and almost surely. In addition, we show that the sequence of iterates of our method almost surely converges to a solution of the underlying problem. We demonstrate the applicability of our method using general error bound criteria, covering mini-batch stochastic estimators as well as three well-known control variate estimators: loopless SVRG, SAGA, and loopless SARAH, for which the last three variants attain significantly better oracle complexity compared to existing methods. We validate our framework and theoretical results through two numerical examples. The preliminary results illustrate promising performance of our accelerated method over its non-accelerated counterparts.



∎

MSC
90C25 90-08

1
Introduction

\mathrm{1.1.}
Generalized equation and special cases.

The goal of this paper is to develop an
accelerated optimistic gradient-type framework with variance reduction
to solve the following
generalized equation
:

\textrm{Find $x^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}$ such that:~ $0\in\Phi{x}^{\star}$ with $\Phi{x}:=G{x}+Tx$},

(GE)

where
G:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
is a single-valued mapping and
T:\mathbb{R}^{p}\rightrightarrows 2^{\mathbb{R}^{p}}
is a possibly multi-valued mapping from
\mathbb{R}^{p}
to
2^{\mathbb{R}^{p}}
, the set of all subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{p}
.
Throughout this paper, we assume that
\mathrm{zer}(\Phi):=\{x^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\mid 0\in\Phi{x}^{\star}\}\neq\emptyset
, i.e., (
GE
) has a solution.

Following
Rockafellar2004
, we refer to (
GE
) as a composite
generalized equation
, but it is also known as a composite
[non]linear inclusion
, see
Bauschke2011
.
In particular, if
T=0
, then (
GE
) reduces to the following [non]linear equation (or also known as a
root-finding problem
):

\textrm{Find $x^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}$ such that:~ $G{x^{\star}}=0$}.

(NE)

This equation is equivalent to the following fixed-point problem:

\textrm{Find $x^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}$ such that $x^{\star}=Fx^{\star}$, where $Fx:=x-\lambda Gx$ for any $\lambda>0$}.

(FP)

However, under appropriate conditions (e.g., maximal [co-hypo]monotonicity of
T
), we can write (
GE
) equivalently to (
NE
) using, e.g., a forward-backward splitting, backward-forward splitting, or a Tseng’s forward-backward-forward splitting operator, see, e.g.,
Bauschke2011
;
TranDinh2025a
.
Under such conditions, we have the following relations: (
GE
)
\Leftrightarrow
(
NE
)
\Leftrightarrow
(
FP
).

Problem (
GE
) looks simple, but it is well-known that (
GE
) covers the optimality condition of unconstrained and constrained minimization, minimax, and [mixed] variational inequality problems.
It also comprises Nash’s equilibrium problems in economics
Facchinei2003
.

\mathrm{1.2.}
Our problem classes and assumptions.

Before specifying our assumptions, we highlight that we will focus on two classes of (
GE
) as follows:

1.

Class 1.

F
is
L
-Lipschitz continuous
and
F+T
is
\rho
-co-hypomonotone
, i.e.,
F
satisfies Assumption
2
with
\alpha=1
and
F+T
satisfies Assumption
4
with
\rho_{n}\geq 0
and
\rho_{c}=0
.

2.

Class 2.

F
satisfies
Assumption
2
with any
\alpha\in[0,1)
and
F+T
satisfy both
Assumption
3
and Assumption
4
with
\rho_{n}\geq 0
and
\rho_{c}>0
.

In both classes, we assume that we do not have access to the operator
G
in (
GE
), but only an unbiased oracle
\mathbf{G}_{\xi}
of
G
.
More specifically, we impose the following assumptions.

Assumption 1

G
is equipped with an unbiased stochastic oracle
\mathbf{G}_{\xi}
such that:

Gx=\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\big[\mathbf{G}_{\xi}x\big],\quad\forall x\in\mathrm{dom}(G).

(1)

Moreover, there exists
\sigma\geq 0
such that
\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\big[\|\mathbf{G}_{\xi}x-Gx\|^{2}\big]\leq\sigma^{2}
.

Assumption
1
is widely used in the literature, especially in optimization, see, e.g.,
lan2020first
;
Nemirovski2009
.
If we choose
\mathbf{G}_{\xi}x:=\frac{1}{n\mathbf{p}_{i}}G_{i}x
for
\mathbf{p}_{i}:=\mathbb{P}(\xi=i)\in(0,1)
, where
G_{i}:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
for
i\in[n]:=\{1,\cdots,n\}
are single-valued mappings, then
G
becomes

Gx:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}G_{i}x.

(2)

This form is called a finite-sum, often obtained by sample average approximation techniques.
In many applications,
n
is very large so that evaluating
G
is expensive or even infeasible.

Assumption 2
(Lipschitz continuity-type)

There exist two constants
L\in(0,+\infty)
and
\alpha\in[0,1]
such that for all
x,y\in\mathrm{dom}(G)
, we have

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\alpha\|Gx-Gy\|^{2}+(1-\alpha)\mathbb{E}\big[\|\mathbf{G}_{\xi}x-\mathbf{G}_{\xi}y\|^{2}\big]\leq L^{2}\|x-y\|^{2}.\end{array}

(3)

Note that we allow
\alpha=0
and
\alpha=1
in Assumption
2
.
If
\alpha=1
, then (
3
) reduces to
\|Gx-Gy\|\leq L\|x-y\|
, showing that
G
is
L
-Lipchitz continuous.
If
\alpha=0
, then (
3
) becomes
\mathbb{E}\big[\|\mathbf{G}_{\xi}x-\mathbf{G}_{\xi}y\|^{2}\big]\leq L^{2}\|x-y\|^{2}
, showing that
G
is
L
-Lipschitz continuous in expectation.
While the former is standard in classical stochastic approximation methods, the latter condition is often used in variance-reduction algorithms with control variate techniques, especially in optimization, see, e.g.,
driggs2019accelerating
;
nguyen2017sarah
;
Pham2019
;
Tran-Dinh2019a
.
In fact, (
3
) is a convex combination of these two extreme cases.
Note that
\|Gx-Gy\|^{2}=\|\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbf{G}_{\xi}x-\mathbf{G}_{\xi}y\big]\|^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}\big[\|\mathbf{G}_{\xi}x-\mathbf{G}_{\xi}y\|^{2}\big]
by Jensen’s inequality, (
3
) implies
\|Gx-Gy\|\leq L\|x-y\|
for all
x,y\in\mathrm{dom}(G)
, i.e.,
G
is
L
-Lipschitz continuous.
In the finite-sum setting (
2
), (
3
) reduces to

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\alpha\|Gx-Gy\|^{2}+\frac{1-\alpha}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|G_{i}x-G_{i}y\|^{2}\leq L^{2}\|x-y\|^{2}.\end{array}

(4)

If
\alpha=0
, then
G
is said to be
L
-average Lipschitz continuous.

Assumption 3
(Weak Minty solution)

There exists a solution
x^{\star}\in\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)
of (
GE
) and a constant
\rho_{*}\geq 0
such that

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\langle w_{x},x-x^{\star}\rangle\geq-\rho_{*}\|w_{x}\|^{2},\quad\forall(x,w_{x})\in\mathrm{gra}(\Phi).\end{array}

(5)

This assumption also covers a class of nonmonotone operators
\Phi
and broadly used in the literature in the last few years, see, e.g.,
diakonikolas2021efficient
;
tran2024revisiting
as a few examples.
It is weaker than the so-called “star-monotonicity” of
\Phi
(i.e.,
\rho_{*}=0
) used in some recent works, e.g.,
gorbunov2022extragradient
;
kotsalis2022simple
.

Assumption 4
(Co-hypomonotonicity-type)

There exist
\rho_{n}\geq 0
and
\rho_{c}\geq 0
such that
\rho_{n}\geq\rho_{c}
and for all
(x,w_{x}),(y,w_{y})\in\mathrm{gra}(\Phi)
, we have

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\langle w_{x}-w_{y},x-y\rangle\geq-\rho_{n}\|w_{x}-w_{y}\|^{2}+\rho_{c}\mathbb{E}\big[\|\mathbf{G}_{\xi}x-\mathbf{G}_{\xi}y\|^{2}\big].\end{array}

(6)

The condition (
6
) in Assumption
4
looks technical and nonstandard.
It deserves further discussion.
Note that since we allow
\rho_{c}=0
, (
6
) can reduce to

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\langle w_{x}-w_{y},x-y\rangle\geq-\rho_{n}\|w_{x}-w_{y}\|^{2}.\end{array}

This relation shows that
\Phi
is
\rho_{n}
-co-hypomonotone
.
Therefore, our assumption is more general than the
\rho_{n}
-co-hypomonotonicity of
\Phi
.
To develop variance-reduction methods via control variate techniques in Section
4
, we will require
\rho_{c}>0
.
In the finite-sum setting (
2
), (
6
) reduces to
\langle w_{x}-w_{y},x-y\rangle\geq-\rho_{n}\|w_{x}-w_{y}\|^{2}+\frac{\rho_{c}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|G_{i}x-G_{i}y\|^{2}
.

In particular, let us discuss the case when
T=0
and
\rho_{c}>0
.
Then, (
6
) reduces to

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\langle Gx-Gy,x-y\rangle\geq-\rho_{n}\|Gx-Gy\|^{2}+\rho_{c}\mathbb{E}\big[\|\mathbf{G}_{\xi}x-\mathbf{G}_{\xi}y\|^{2}\big],\quad\forall x,y\in\mathrm{dom}(G).\end{array}

(7)

We have the following facts corresponding to
G
in this case.

•

First, if
\mathbf{G}
is deterministic, i.e.,
Gx\equiv\mathbf{G}_{\xi}x
in (
1
) or
n=1
in (
2
), then (
7
) reduces to
\langle Gx-Gy,x-y\rangle\geq-\rho\|Gx-Gy\|^{2}
for
\rho:=\rho_{n}-\rho_{c}
.
Clearly, if
\rho_{n}<\rho_{c}
, then
G
is
|\rho|
-co-coercive.
If
\rho_{c}=\rho_{n}
, then
G
is monotone.
If
\rho_{n}>\rho_{c}
, then
G
is
\rho
-co-hypomonotone (see Subsection
2.1
for the definition of these concepts).

•

Second, since
\mathbb{E}\big[\|\mathbf{G}_{\xi}x-\mathbf{G}_{\xi}y\|^{2}\big]\geq\|\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbf{G}_{\xi}x-\mathbf{G}_{\xi}y\big]\|^{2}
by Jensen’s inequality, (
7
) implies that
\langle Gx-Gy,x-y\rangle\geq-\rho\|Gx-Gy\|^{2}
for
\rho:=\rho_{n}-\rho_{c}
.
Therefore, if
\rho_{n}<\rho_{c}
, then (
7
) is slightly stronger than the
|\rho|
-co-coercivity of
G
.
If
\rho_{n}>\rho_{c}
, then (
7
) is slightly stronger than the
\rho
-co-hypomonotonicity of
G
.

•

Third, since
\rho_{n}\geq 0
, if
\rho_{c}>0
, then (
7
) is considerably weaker than the
\rho_{c}
-average co-coercivity of
G
, i.e.,
\langle Gx-Gy,x-y\rangle\geq\rho_{c}\mathbb{E}\big[\|\mathbf{G}_{\xi}x-\mathbf{G}_{\xi}y\|^{2}\big]
.
This
\rho_{c}
-average
-co-coercivity assumption has widely been used in many recent works to develop variance reduction methods for (
GE
) and its special cases, see, e.g.,
cai2023variance
;
davis2022variance
;
tran2024accelerated
;
TranDinh2025a
.

Figure 1:

Different classes of operators
G
.
The class “
A.3.
(\rho_{c}>0
)
” represents the case
G
satisfying (
7
) with
\rho_{c}>0
.
The class “
A.3.
(\rho_{c}=0
)
” covers all
co-hypomonotone operators
.

In this paper,
we do not focus on the case
\rho_{n}<\rho_{c}
in (
7
)
because
G
is
(\rho_{c}-\rho_{n})
-co-coercive, which has been studied in previous works, including our papers
tran2024accelerated
;
TranDinh2025a
.

We only consider the case
\rho_{n}\geq\rho_{c}\geq 0
, which covers a class of monotone and co-hypomonotone operators.
Figure
1
illustrates the class of
\rho
-co-hypomonotone operators
G
as well as the class of
G
satisfying condition (
6
) when
\rho_{c}>0
.
The latter intersects with both monotone and nonmonotone operators.
Subsection
2.4
provides many examples of
\Phi
and
G
satisfying (
6
) and (
7
).

Note that if
T=0
, then Assumption
4
implies the
\rho
-co-hypomonotonicity of
G
with
\rho:=\rho_{n}-\rho_{c}
.
Thus, Assumption
3
automatically holds with
\rho_{*}=\rho
.
Alternatively, if
\rho_{c}=0
, then Assumption
4
also implies the
\rho_{n}
-co-hypomonotonicity of
\Phi
, and hence, Assumption
3
also automatically holds with
\rho_{*}=\rho_{n}
.
In these two cases, Assumption
3
is unnecessary and can be removed.

1.2. Motivation and challenges.

Both minimax and variational inequality problems are fundamental and powerful tools for modeling two-person games, Nash equilibria, and robust optimization
Bauschke2011
;
reginaset2008
;
Facchinei2003
;
phelps2009convex
;
Rockafellar2004
;
Rockafellar1976b
;
ryu2016primer
, and more recently, generative adversarial learning, online learning, reinforcement learning, and distributionally robust optimization
arjovsky2017wasserstein
;
azar2017minimax
;
Ben-Tal2009
;
bhatia2020online
;
goodfellow2014generative
;
jabbar2021survey
;
levy2020large
;
lin2022distributionally
;
madry2018towards
;
rahimian2019distributionally
;
wei2021last
.
Their optimality conditions can be expressed as a generalized equation (
GE
).
Yet, unlike classical models, modern applications are data-driven, large-scale, and often involve nonconvex, nonmonotone, and/or nonsmooth structures, limiting the use of deterministic and primal-dual approaches.
Mathematically, these problems are typically formulated as large finite-sums or as stochastic expectations induced by sampled data.
Consequently, contemporary methods face significant challenges, including restrictive assumptions, high computational operation cost, low efficiency, and slow convergence.

Hitherto, most methods developed for (
GE
) and its special cases are deterministic and primarily address monotone problems, see, e.g.,
Bauschke2011
;
Facchinei2003
;
Konnov2001
.
They are inefficient for the finite-sum setting (
2
) when
n
is large, and inapplicable to the expectation setting (
1
), where
G
is not directly accessible.
Randomized and stochastic methods exist for (
GE
), but under restrictive assumptions
boct2021minibatch
;
combettes2015stochastic
;
cui2021analysis
;
kotsalis2022simple
;
pethick2023solving
.
Many of these still rely on the classical Robbins-Monro approach without acceleration, resulting in slow convergence rates and oracle complexities often ranging from
\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-6}\big)
to
\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-4}\big)
, depending on the method and assumptions.

Recently, several works have applied variance-reduction techniques using control variates, commonly developed in optimization, to (
GE
), improving oracle complexity over the classical stochastic approximation.
However, these algorithms are non-accelerated with slow convergence rates, typically
\mathcal{O}\big(1/k\big)
, see e.g.,
alacaoglu2021stochastic
;
alacaoglu2021forward
;
davis2022variance
.
While
alacaoglu2021stochastic
;
alacaoglu2021forward
rely on Lipschitz continuity of
G
and monotonicity of
\Phi
, others such as
cai2023variance
;
davis2022variance
;
tran2024accelerated
;
TranDinh2025a
assume stronger conditions: co-coercivity and/or strong quasi-monotonicity.
Co-coercivity is strong and hard to verify in practice, it is stronger than monotonicity plus Lipschitz continuity and fails for a bilinear mapping
Fx=[\mathbf{L}v,-\mathbf{L}^{T}u]
, where
x=(u,v)
and
\mathbf{L}
is given.
Moreover, checking co-coercivity of the saddle mapping in minimax problems is non-trivial, even in the convex-concave case.

\mathrm{1.3.}
Our goal and approach.

The above shortcomings raise the following research question:

Can we develop a single-loop algorithmic framework capable of achieving faster
\mathcal{O}\big(1/k^{2}\big)
and
o\big(1/k^{2}\big)
convergence rates via acceleration, covering a wide range of variants
(
using both unbiased and biased estimators
)
, attaining improved oracle complexity through variance reduction, and requiring weaker assumptions
(
including a subclass of nonmonotone problems
)
?

This paper aims at addressing this question.
However, we face two main technical challenges.
First, classical Krasnosel’kiǐ-Mann (KM) iteration is no longer applicable, requiring a shift to extragradient-type (EG) schemes.
Nevertheless, EG involves a composition evaluation
G(x-\eta Gx)
, which complicates for designing and analyzing stochastic algorithms.
We instead exploit Popov’s past-extragradient method
popov1980modification
(also closely related to the optimistic gradient scheme) to overcome this limitation.
Second, developing variance-reduction methods with control variate techniques requires a structural assumption on
\Phi
, often called “average co-coercivity”
cai2023variance
;
davis2022variance
;
tran2024accelerated
;
TranDinh2025a
, which excludes certain problem classes, particularly nonmonotone ones. We identify a class of problems at the intersection of monotone and nonmonotone operators that we can handle, typically satisfying Assumptions
2
–
4
.

Our algorithmic development relies on three main ideas: the optimistic gradient method, Nesterov’s acceleration, and variance-reduction techniques.
While each has been studied extensively, their unified combination to solve (
GE
) under Assumptions
2
–
4
remains largely unexplored, especially for nonmonotone problems.

\mathrm{1.4.}
Our contributions.

To this end, our contributions consist of the following:

\mathrm{(a)}

First, we introduce Assumption
4
to define a new class of problems (
GE
), complementing the standard assumptions: Assumptions
1
–
3
.
We illustrate its plausibility with concrete examples, showing that the resulting class lies at the intersection of monotone and nonmonotone operators, possibly covers monotone operators (see Section
2.4
).

\mathrm{(b)}

Second, we develop a novel stochastic fast optimistic gradient framework to approximate solutions of (
GE
).
Our framework only requires one stochastic approximation
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
for
Gy^{k}
per iteration, evaluated at the iterate
y^{k}
.
We introduce an error bound condition between
Gy^{k}
and its stochastic variance-reduced estimator
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
(unbiased or biased) via its stochastic oracle
\mathbf{G}_{\xi}
used for our convergence analysis.
Alternatively, we also propose a class of control-variate-based estimators for
G
for our framework, covering both unbiased and biased instances, including SVRG
SVRG
, SAGA
Defazio2014
, and SARAH
nguyen2017sarah
.

\mathrm{(c)}

Third, we establish
\mathcal{O}\big(1/k^{2}\big)
convergence rates in expectation for
\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]
using either the proposed error bound criterion or our class of variance-reduced estimators, where
x^{k}
is the iterate and
v^{k}\in Tx^{k}
. We further prove
o\big(1/k^{2}\big)
convergence of
\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}
both in expectation and almost surely, and show that the sequences
\{x^{k}\}
and
\{y^{k}\}
both converge almost surely to a random variable
x^{\star}\in\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)
, a solution of (
GE
).

\mathrm{(d)}

Finally, we demonstrate our framework with three common estimators: Loopless SVRG, SAGA, and Loopless SARAH, and analyze their oracle complexities for the finite-sum case (
2
).
For Loopless SVRG and SAGA, we obtain
\mathcal{O}\big(n\log(n)+n^{2/3}\epsilon^{-1}\big)
, while for Loopless SARAH we achieve
\mathcal{O}\big(n\log(n)+n^{1/2}\epsilon^{-1}\big)
, where
\epsilon>0
is the target accuracy.

We believe that our framework is simple to implement, as it is single-loop.
Moreover, it includes a broad class of variance-reduced and accelerated algorithms, covering mini-batch, unbiased, and biased estimators, which is also rarely seen in optimization.
Unlike most existing methods limited to the monotone setting or special cases of (
GE
), our approach can handle a class of nonmonotone (
GE
).
Its convergence guarantees are notable, covering
\mathcal{O}\big(1/k^{2}\big)
and
o\big(1/k^{2}\big)
convergence rates, and almost sure convergence of iterates.
The latter two have been scarcely studied, even in variance-reduction methods for convex optimization.

\mathrm{1.5.}
Related work.

Problem (
GE
) is classical, with its theory and numerical methods extensively studied over decades, as presented in monographs such as
Bauschke2011
;
Facchinei2003
;
minty1962monotone
;
Rockafellar1997
and references therein.
In this paper, we focus on a different class of (
GE
) and develop novel stochastic single-loop methods with acceleration and variance reduction.
Accordingly, we restrict our review and comparison to works along this direction.

Gap
is the metric
\mathcal{G}(x):=\max_{y\in\mathcal{B}}\langle Gy,y-x\rangle
;
and
an unbiased [and biased] class
= a class of unbiased [and biased] variance-reduced estimators.

\mathrm{(a)}
From non-accelerated to accelerated methods.

The key advantage of accelerated methods over non-accelerated ones is their faster convergence rates:
\mathcal{O}\big(1/k^{2}\big)
vs.
\mathcal{O}\big(1/k\big)
.
Nesterov first introduced acceleration for smooth convex optimization
Nesterov1983
, later extended to other problems and monotone inclusions, including (
GE
)
attouch2020convergence
;
attouch2019convergence
. This line of research has recently gained strong attention, especially in deterministic settings
bot2022fast
;
bot2022bfast
;
kim2021accelerated
;
mainge2021accelerated
;
tran2021halpern
;
tran2025accelerated
.

As an alternative, Halpern’s fixed-point iteration
halpern1967fixed
also achieves accelerated
\mathcal{O}\big(1/k^{2}\big)
rates on the squared norm of residual for (
GE
) under co-coercivity
diakonikolas2020halpern
;
lieder2021convergence
;
sabach2017first
, improving on the classical
\mathcal{O}\big(1/k\big)
rate of the Krasnosel’kiǐ-Mann (KM) method and its variants.

These advances have driven extensive work in operator theory, mostly in deterministic settings
cai2022accelerated
;
cai2022baccelerated
;
lee2021fast
;
tran2021halpern
;
yoon2021accelerated
. Though Nesterov- and Halpern-type accelerations were developed independently, their connection was later established in
tran2022connection
and also
partkryu2022
.
In this paper, we build on Nesterov’s acceleration to design stochastic variance-reduction algorithms for (
GE
) under Assumptions
2
–
4
and establish their rigorous convergence guarantees.

\mathrm{(b)}
From convex optimization to generalized equations.

In convex optimization, the
L
-smoothness of the objective function is equivalent to its gradient co-coercivity
Bauschke2011
;
Nesterov2004
, making smooth convex minimization problems special cases of (
NE
) with
G
being co-coercive.
By contrast, smooth convex-concave minimax problems often lack this property (see, e.g., bilinear game models
Facchinei2003
), highlighting a key distinction between optimization and generalized equations (
GE
). This shift complicates algorithm design and convergence analysis, as many standard analysis tools in convex optimization no longer apply to (
GE
)
attouch2019convergence
;
kim2021accelerated
;
tran2022connection
.
One technical challenge is the absence of an objective function in (
GE
), complicating the construction of a suitable Lyapunov function that underpins convergence guarantees in accelerated methods.
In addition, unlike optimization, developing accelerated randomized and variance-reduced methods for data-driven (
GE
) remains challenging but crucial.

Beyond co-coercivity, extragradient (EG) and its variants
Korpelevic1976
;
popov1980modification
;
daskalakis2018training
are prominent, but their stochastic extensions face difficulties: dependence on intermediate updates
yu2022fast
and the breakdown of gap function-based analysis under nonmonotonicity
alacaoglu2021stochastic
;
alacaoglu2021forward
.
Dual averaging and mirror descent
Nemirovskii2004
;
kotsalis2022simple
are similarly restricted to monotone problems.
In this work, we extend Popov’s EG variant to the stochastic setting, addressing cases with biased estimators.

\mathrm{(c)}
Existing stochastic methods.

Stochastic methods for (
GE
) often follow the Robbins-Monro stochastic approximation framework, but converge slowly up to a
\mathcal{O}\big(1/k\big)
rate with inefficient oracle complexity, see, e.g.,
boob2023first
;
censor2012extensions
;
cui2021analysis
;
jiang2008stochastic
;
juditsky2011solving
;
kannan2019optimal
;
koshal2012regularized
.
Recent work improves this limitation via variance reduction techniques, e.g., adaptive or growing mini-batches
boct2021minibatch
;
pethick2023solving
.
Alternative approaches such as dual averaging and mirror descent
chen2017accelerated
;
kotsalis2022simple
extend to VIPs but require monotonicity, limiting applicability.
Other advances include stochastic splitting and mirror-prox methods
beznosikov2023stochastic
;
gorbunov2022stochastic
;
loizou2021stochastic
.
In contrast, our work employs accelerated methods with a broad class of variance-reduced estimators, including several well-known instances.

\mathrm{(d)}
Variance-reduction methods using control variate techniques.

In the co-coercive setting, several variance-reduced methods using control variates have been proposed
cai2023variance
;
davis2022variance
;
tran2024accelerated
;
TranDinh2025a
, exploiting co-coercivity for faster rates and improved oracle complexity. Table
1
summarizes recent works by assumptions, estimators, and guarantees.
Beyond co-coercivity, EG variants, often based on Popov’s scheme
popov1980modification
, have been studied for monotone VIPs.
Examples include SVRG-based methods in
alacaoglu2021stochastic
;
alacaoglu2021forward
;
yu2022fast
, which use gap functions for convergence analysis and rely on bounded-variance or unbiased estimators.

Our approach keeps the simple single-loop structure as methods in
alacaoglu2021stochastic
;
alacaoglu2021forward
;
cai2023variance
;
davis2022variance
but (i) adds acceleration, (ii) applies to “co-hypomonotone-type” operators, (iii) handles both unbiased and biased estimators with general error bound conditions, and (iv) achieves stronger convergence properties and oracle complexity (iv) under weaker assumptions.
Unlike
alacaoglu2021stochastic
;
yu2022fast
, our method provides residual guarantees with better complexity for the finite-sum case.

\mathrm{1.6.}
Paper organization.

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows.
Section
2
reviews the necessary background and concepts, and presents examples of
\Phi
and
G
satisfying Assumption
4
.
Section
3
develops a generic fast optimistic gradient framework with variance reduction to solve (
1
) of (
GE
) under Assumptions
2
–
4
, establishing convergence under a general error bound condition.
Section
4
introduces an alternative variant using control-variate-based variance reduction to handle both (
1
) and (
2
), with similar convergence guarantees.
Section
5
provides a complete convergence analysis of the proposed methods.
Section
6
presents two numerical examples to validate our theoretical results and benchmark against state-of-the-art methods.
Technical results and proofs are deferred to the appendix.

2
Background, Preliminaries, and Examples

We recall necessary notations and concepts used in this paper, and briefly describe Popov’s method.
Finally, we provide different examples to illustrate Assumption
4
.

2.1
Notations and concepts

Basic notations.

We work with finite-dimensional spaces
\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n}
equipped with standard inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
and norm
\|\cdot\|
.
For a function
f:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\nabla{f}
denotes the gradient of
f
,
\partial{f}
denotes the subdifferential of
f
, and
\mathrm{prox}_{f}
is the proximal operator of
f
.
For a given random vector
\mathbf{u}_{\xi}
in
\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
v\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, we define
u:=\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\big[\mathbf{u}_{\xi}\big]
and
|\!\|u+v|\!\|^{2}:=\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\big[\|\mathbf{u}_{\xi}+v\|^{2}\big]
.
By Jensen’s inequality, we also have
|\!\|u+v|\!\|^{2}\geq\|u+v\|^{2}
.
For a given symmetric matrix
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
,
\mathbf{Q}\succeq 0
(resp.,
\mathbf{Q}\succ 0
) means that
\mathbf{Q}
is positive semidefinite (resp., positive definite).
For two symmetric matrices
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbf{Q}
, the relation
\mathbf{P}\succeq\mathbf{Q}
means that
\mathbf{P}-\mathbf{Q}\succeq 0
.
For given functions
g(t)
and
h(t)
, we say that
g(t)=\mathcal{O}\big(h(t)\big)
if there exist
M>0
and
t_{0}\geq 0
such that
g(t)\leq Mh(t)
for
t\geq t_{0}
.
We use
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(g(t))
to hide a poly-log factor of
g(t)
.

Filtration and expectations.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{k}
denote the
\sigma
-algebra generated by all the randomness of our algorithm up to iteration
k
, including the iterates
x^{0},\cdots,x^{k}
.
We also denote by
\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\cdot\big]:=\mathbb{E}\big[\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}\big]
the conditional expectation conditioned on
\mathcal{F}_{k}
, and by
\mathbb{E}\big[\cdot\big]
the full expectation.
For a random variable
\xi
, we denote by
\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\big[\cdot\big]
the expectation w.r.t.
\xi
.

Lipschitz continuity.

For a single-valued mapping
G:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
, we say that
G
is
L
-Lipschitz continuous if there exists
L\geq 0
such that
\|Gx-Gy\|\leq L\|x-y\|
for all
x,y\in\mathrm{dom}(G)
.
If
L<1
, then
G
is contractive, and if
L=1
, then
G
is nonexpansive.

Co-monotonicity, monotonicity, and co-coercivity.

For a single-valued or multi-valued mapping
G:\mathbb{R}^{p}\rightrightarrows 2^{\mathbb{R}^{p}}
(where
2^{\mathbb{R}^{p}}
is the set of all subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{p}
), we denote by
\mathrm{dom}(G):=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}:Gx\neq\emptyset\}
the domain of
G
and by
\mathrm{gra}(G):=\{(x,u)\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}:u\in Gx\}
the graph of
G
.
If
G
is differentiable at
x
, then
G^{\prime}(x)
denotes its derivative at
x
.
We say that
\mathrm{gra}(G)
of
G
is closed if for any sequence
\{(x^{k},u^{k})\}
such that
(x^{k},u^{k})\in\mathrm{gra}(G)
and
(x^{k},u^{k})\to(x,u)
as
k\to\infty
, then
(x,u)\in\mathrm{gra}(G)
.
For further details of these concepts, we refer to
Bauschke2011
.

Definition 1

Let
G:\mathbb{R}^{p}\to 2^{\mathbb{R}^{p}}
be a given single-valued or multi-valued mapping.

•

We say that
G
is monotone if for all
(x,u),(y,v)\in\mathrm{gra}(G)
, we have
\langle u-v,x-y\rangle\geq 0
.

•

We say that
G
is
\rho
-co-monotone if there exists
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\langle u-v,x-y\rangle\geq\rho\|u-v\|^{2}
for all
(x,u),(y,v)\in\mathrm{gra}(G)
.
Clearly, if
\rho=0
, then
G
reduces to a monotone mapping.
See
bauschke2020generalized
for the definitions and further properties.

–

If
\langle u-v,x-y\rangle\geq\rho\|u-v\|^{2}
for
\rho<0
, then we say that
G
is
|\rho|
-co-hypomonotone.
A
|\rho|
-co-hypomonotone mapping can be nonmonotone (see examples, e.g., in
TranDinh2025a
).

–

If
\langle u-v,x-y\rangle\geq\rho\|u-v\|^{2}
for
\rho>0
, then we say that
G
is
\rho
-co-coercive.
Moreover, if
G
is
\rho
-co-coercive, then it is also monotone and
L
-Lipschitz continuous with
L:=\frac{1}{\rho}
.

If
f
is a convex and
L
-smooth (i.e.,
\nabla{f}
is
L
-Lipschitz continuous) function, then
\nabla{f}
is
\frac{1}{L}
-co-coercive.
The
\rho
-co-coercivity of a mapping
G
is also equivalent to the nonexpansiveness of
F=\mathbb{I}-2\rho G
in the fixed-point problem (
FP
), see
Bauschke2011
.
All the concepts defined above are global.
If
G
satisfies any of the above property for all
(x,u),(y,v)\in\mathrm{gra}(G)\cap\mathcal{W}
, where
\mathcal{W}
is a neighborhood of
(\bar{x},\bar{u})
, then we say that
G
has such a local property around
(\bar{x},\bar{u})
.

2.2
The past extragradient method

One classical and well-known method for solving (
GE
) under the Lipschitz continuity of
G
and the monotonicity of
G
and
T=\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{X}}
(the normal cone of
\mathcal{X}
) is the extragradient method, introduced by G. M. Korpelevich in
Korpelevic1976
in 1976 (also by A. S. Antipin in
antipin1976
(1976)).
This method has inspired various variants, among which the past-extragradient method, a notable variant proposed by Popov in
popov1980modification
, is particularly well-known.
When applied to solving (
GE
), this variant is equivalent to the optimistic gradient method studied in
daskalakis2018training
;
mertikopoulos2019optimistic
;
mokhtari2020convergence
.

This method can be briefly described as follows:
Starting from
x^{0}\in\mathrm{dom}(\Phi)
, set
y^{-1}:=x^{0}
, and at each iteration
k\geq 0
, we update

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}y^{k}&:=&J_{\eta T}(x^{k}-\eta Gy^{k-1}),\\
x^{k+1}&:=&J_{\eta T}(x^{k}-\eta Gy^{k}),\end{array}\right.

(PEG)

where
\eta>0
is a given stepsize.
This scheme have been well-studied in the literature, including
malitsky2020forward
;
popov1980modification
;
tran2024revisiting
, mostly in the deterministic setting.
It also has closed connection to other methods such as projected reflected gradient
malitsky2015projected
, forward-reflected-backward splitting
malitsky2020forward
, golden-ratio
malitsky2019golden
methods.
As mentioned, when
T=0
, it is also equivalent to the optimistic gradient method
daskalakis2018training
;
mertikopoulos2019optimistic
;
mokhtari2020convergence
.
Inspired by (
PEG
), we develop an accelerated algorithmic framework with variance reduction to solve (
GE
) under Assumptions
1
-
4
.

2.3
Examples of co-hypomonotone operators

As shown in
TranDinh2025a
, given a
p\times p
symmetric and invertible matrix
\mathbf{G}
and a vector
\mathbf{g}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
, a linear mapping
Gx:=\mathbf{G}x+\mathbf{g}
is
\rho
-co-monotone with
\rho:=\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{G}^{-1})
.
If
\rho:=\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{G}^{-1})<0
, then
G
is nonmonotone, but
|\rho|
-co-hypomonotone.
Generally, if there exists
\rho\geq 0
such that
\mathbf{G}+\mathbf{G}^{\top}+2\rho\mathbf{G}^{\top}\mathbf{G}\succeq 0
, then
G
is
\rho
-co-hypomonotone, where
\mathbf{G}
is not necessarily symmetric.
As a conjecture, we believe that several piecewise linear mappings are also
\rho
-co-hypomonotone, or at least locally
\rho
-co-hypomonotone.
They often represent the subdifferential of nonconvex regularizers broadly used in statistical learning and machine learning.

As a concrete example, one can prove that the subdifferential
Tx=\partial{\textrm{SCAD}}(x)
of the SCAD (smoothly clipped absolute deviation) regularizer in
fan2001variable
, widely employed in statistics, is not globally
\rho
-co-hypomonotone, but locally
\rho
-co-hypomonotone with
\rho=2.7
.

As proven in
(
evens2023convergence
, Proposition 4.17)
, the saddle mapping
\Phi{x}:=[\nabla_{u}\Psi(u,v),-\nabla_{v}\Psi(u,v)]
is
\rho
-co-hypomonotone if
\Psi
is
\alpha
-interaction dominate, a concept introduced in
grimmer2023landscape
for studying nonconvex-nonconcave smooth minimax problems
\min_{u}\max_{v}\Psi(u,v)
, where
\alpha
relates to
\rho
.
In fact, the co-hypomonotonicity was generalized to a so-called
semi-monotonicity
in
evens2023convergence
, where the authors provided various examples and established further properties.

2.4
Examples of co-hypomonotone operators satisfying Assumption
4

The Lipschitz continuity-type condition (
3
) in Assumption
2
is common, especially when
\alpha=0
or
\alpha=1
.
Let us provide concrete examples that satisfy our “co-hypomonotonicity-type” condition (
6
) in Assumption
4
and condition (
7
) (i.e.,
T=0
).

\mathrm{(a)}

Example 1
(
Concrete linear mappings
).
We first consider a concrete linear example.
Given three square matrices
\mathbf{G}_{1}
,
\mathbf{G}_{2}
, and
\hat{\mathbf{G}}
, and two vectors
\mathbf{g}_{1}
and
\mathbf{g}_{2}
as follows:

\mathbf{G}_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}-2&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix},\quad\mathbf{G}_{2}=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},\quad\hat{\mathbf{G}}=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&1/2\end{bmatrix},\quad\mathbf{g}_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix},\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathbf{g}_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}-1\\
1\end{pmatrix}.

(8)

We define three mappings
G_{1}x:=\mathbf{G}_{1}x+\mathbf{g}_{1}
,
G_{2}x:=\mathbf{G}_{2}x+\mathbf{g}_{2}
, and
Tx:=\hat{\mathbf{G}}x
.
Then, we have
Gx:=\frac{1}{2}(G_{1}x+G_{2}x)
and
\Phi{x}:=Gx+Tx
.
Since
\mathbf{\Phi}:=\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{G}_{1}+\mathbf{G}_{2})+\hat{\mathbf{G}}=\begin{bmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
is not positive semidefinite,
\Phi
is nonmonotone.

Now, for any vector
u\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we can easily check that for some
\rho_{n}\geq\rho_{c}>0
, we have

\begin{array}[]{lcl}u^{\top}\mathbf{\Phi}u\geq-\rho_{n}\|\mathbf{\Phi}u\|^{2}+\frac{\rho_{c}}{2}\big(\|\mathbf{G}_{1}u\|^{2}+\|\mathbf{G}_{2}u\|^{2}\big).\end{array}

(9)

Since
\mathbf{\Phi}
is symmetric, the condition (
9
) is equivalent to

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbf{\Phi}+\rho_{n}\mathbf{\Phi}^{\top}\mathbf{\Phi}-\frac{\rho_{c}}{2}\big(\mathbf{G}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{1}+\mathbf{G}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{2}\big)\succeq 0.\end{array}

By a direct calculation, there exist
\rho_{n}:=2\epsilon+1
and
\rho_{c}:=\epsilon>0
for any
\epsilon>0
such that this inequality holds.
Therefore, with these given
\rho_{n}
and
\rho_{c}
, for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we have

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\langle\Phi{x}-{\Phi}y,x-y\rangle\geq-\rho_{n}\|\Phi{x}-\Phi{y}\|^{2}+\frac{\rho_{c}}{2}\big(\|G_{1}x-G_{1}y\|^{2}+\|G_{2}x-G_{2}y\|^{2}\big).\end{array}

Overall, we have shown that the mappings
G
and
T
satisfy Assumption
4
.

\mathrm{(b)}

Example 2
(
A class of (
GE
) studied in
tran2024accelerated
;
TranDinh2025a
).
In
tran2024accelerated
;
TranDinh2025a
, the author studied a class of (
GE
) in which
G
is
\frac{1}{L}
-co-coercive in expectation, i.e., for all
x,y\in\mathrm{dom}(G)
, there exists
L>0
such that

\begin{array}[]{l}\langle Gx-Gy,x-y\rangle=\mathbb{E}\big[\langle\mathbf{G}_{\xi}x-\mathbf{G}_{\xi}y,x-y\rangle\big]\geq\frac{1}{L}\mathbb{E}\big[\|\mathbf{G}_{\xi}x-\mathbf{G}_{\xi}y\|^{2}\big],\end{array}

and
T
is
\rho
-maximally co-hypomonotone.

As shown in Appendix
A.2
, one can check that
\Phi:=G+T
satisfies (
6
) in Assumption
4
with
\rho_{n}:=\frac{(1+\tau)\rho}{\tau}\geq 0
and
0\leq\rho_{c}\leq\frac{1}{L}-(1+\tau)\rho
for any
\tau>0
.
Here, to ensure
\rho_{c}\geq 0
, we require
0\leq L\rho\leq\frac{1}{1+\tau}<1
.

\mathrm{(c)}

Example 3
(
General linear mappings
).
Let us consider the following linear mappings:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}G_{i}x:=\mathbf{G}_{i}x+\mathbf{g}_{i}\quad(i\in[n])\quad\textrm{and}\quad Gx:=\mathbf{G}x+\mathbf{g}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}G_{i}x,\end{array}

(10)

where
\mathbf{G}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
and
\mathbf{g}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
for
i\in[n]
are given,
\mathbf{G}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{G}_{i}
, and
\mathbf{g}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{g}_{i}
.
Suppose that there exist
\rho_{n}>0
and
\rho_{c}>0
such that

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{G}+\mathbf{G}^{\top}\big)+\rho_{n}\mathbf{G}^{\top}\mathbf{G}-\frac{\rho_{c}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{G}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{i}\succeq 0.\end{array}

(11)

Then, one can easily show that
G
satisfies the condition (
7
), a special case of the condition (
6
) in Assumption
4
when
T=0
.

If
\lambda_{\min}(\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{G}+\mathbf{G}^{\top}))<0
, then
G
is nonmonotone.
Additionally, if we assume that
\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{G}^{\top}\mathbf{G})>0
(i.e.,
\mathbf{G}
is full-row rank), then we can choose

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\rho_{n}\geq-\frac{\lambda_{\min}(\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{G}+\mathbf{G}^{\top}))}{\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{G}^{\top}\mathbf{G})}>0\quad\textrm{and}\quad 0\leq\rho_{c}\leq\frac{n(\lambda_{\min}(\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{G}+\mathbf{G}^{\top}))+\rho_{n}\lambda_{\min}(\mathbf{G}^{\top}\mathbf{G}))}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{\max}(\mathbf{G}_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{G}_{i})}.\end{array}

(12)

In this case,
\rho_{n}
and
\rho_{c}
chosen as in (
12
) satisfy (
11
).
Consequently, the condition (
7
) (a special case of Assumption
4
when
T=0
) is satisfied.

\mathrm{(d)}

Example 4
(
Bounded differential operator diversity
).
For given
n
operators
G_{i}
, we define its difference diversity ratio:

R(x,y):=\frac{n\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|G_{i}x-G_{i}y\|^{2}}{\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}(G_{i}x-G_{i}y)\|^{2}},\quad\forall x,y\in\mathrm{dom}(G),\ x\neq y.

(13)

This concept is similar to the differential gradient diversity concept in
yin2018gradient
often used in federated learning and distributed optimization.
Here, we extend it to operators.
Since
\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}(G_{i}x-G_{i}y)\|^{2}\leq n\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|G_{i}x-G_{i}y\|^{2}
by Jensen’s inequality, we have
R(x,y)\geq 1
for all
x,y\in\mathrm{dom}(G)
.
Next, we assume that
R(x,y)
is upper bounded for all
x,y\in\mathrm{dom}(G)
, i.e., there exists
M\geq 1
such that
1\leq R(x,y)\leq M
for all
x,y\in\mathrm{dom}(G)
.

Now, suppose that
G
is also
\rho
-co-hypomonotone with
\rho>0
, then it satisfies the condition (
7
) in Assumption
4
with
\rho_{n}>\rho
and
\rho_{c}:=\frac{\rho_{n}-\rho}{M}>0
(see Appendix
A.2
).

\mathrm{(e)}

Example 5
(
Bounded derivatives
).
Suppose that all
G_{i}
for
i\in[n]
are continuously differentiable.
Let
\bar{\sigma}_{i}:=\sup_{x\in\mathrm{dom}(G_{i})}\sigma_{\max}(G_{i}^{\prime}(x))
be the upper bound of the largest singular value of
G_{i}^{\prime}(x)
and
\underline{\sigma}:=\inf_{x\in\mathrm{dom}(G)}\sigma_{\min}(G^{\prime}(x))
be the lower bound of the smallest singular value of
G^{\prime}(x)
.
Denote
\bar{\sigma}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bar{\sigma}_{i}
.
Suppose that

0<\underline{\sigma}\leq\bar{\sigma}<+\infty.

Then, if
G
is additionally
\rho
-co-hypomonotone with
\rho>0
, then it satisfies the condition (
7
) in Assumption
4
with
\rho_{n}>\rho
and
\rho_{c}:=\frac{(\rho_{n}-\rho)\underline{\sigma}^{2}}{\bar{\sigma}^{2}}>0
(see Appendix
A.2
).

3
Variance Reduced Fast Optimistic Gradient Method for General Case

In this section, we exploit three main ideas: Popov’s past-EG scheme, Nesterov’s acceleration, and variance reduction techniques to develop a novel algorithmic framework for solving a class of (
GE
) under the
Lipschitz continuity of
G
and the
\rho_{n}
-co-hypomonotonicity of
\Phi
.
That is: we solve a class of (
GE
) covered by Assumption
2
with
\alpha=1
and Assumption
4
with
\rho_{n}\geq 0
and
\rho_{c}=0
.

3.1
The proposed algorithm

Main idea.

The following variant of Nesterov’s accelerated method from
Beck2009
;
Nesterov1983
for minimizing the sum
\phi:=f+g
of a convex and
L
-smooth function
f
and a possibly nonsmooth and convex function
g
can be expressed as follows using a subgradient
v^{k+1}
of
g
at
x^{k+1}
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hat{x}^{k}&:=&\frac{t_{k}-s}{t_{k}}x^{k}+\frac{s}{t_{k}}z^{k},\\
x^{k+1}&:=&\hat{x}^{k}-\eta(\nabla{f}(\hat{x}^{k})+v^{k+1}),\\
z^{k+1}&:=&z^{k}-\frac{\eta t_{k}}{s}(\nabla{f}(\hat{x}^{k})+v^{k+1}),\end{array}\right.\vskip-2.15277pt

(NAG)

where
v^{k+1}\in\partial{g}(x^{k+1})
,
0<\eta\leq\frac{1}{L}
is a given stepsize, and
t_{k}=k+s+1
for some
s>1
.
This algorithm is known to achieve a
\mathcal{O}\big(1/k^{2}\big)
convergence rate on the objective residual
\phi(x^{k})-\phi(x^{\star})
, significantly improving over the
\mathcal{O}\big(1/k\big)
rate in the standard proximal gradient method.
Under an appropriate choice of
s
, (
NAG
) can achieve up to a
o\big(1/k^{2}\big)
rate
attouch2016rate
.

Our idea is to exploit this (
NAG
) scheme and combine it with Popov’s past-EG mechanism to develop a fast optimistic gradient scheme for (
GE
).
However, since we can only access an unbiased stochastic oracle
\mathbf{G}_{\xi}
of
G
, we will approximate the value
Gy^{k}
by a stochastic variance-reduced estimator
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
constructed from
\mathbf{G}_{\xi}y^{k}
.
This combination leads to the following new
V
ariance-reduced
F
ast
O
ptimistic
G
radient
method, termed by VFOG.

The proposed method.

Given an initial point
x^{0}\in\mathrm{dom}(\Phi)
, we set
z^{0}=y^{-1}:=x^{0}
, and at each iteration
k\geq 0
, we construct a stochastic estimator
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
of
Gy^{k}
, and then update

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hat{x}^{k}&:=&\frac{t_{k}-s}{t_{k}}x^{k}+\frac{s}{t_{k}}z^{k},\\
y^{k}&:=&\hat{x}^{k}-(\eta-\beta_{k})(\widetilde{G}y^{k-1}+v^{k}),\\
x^{k+1}&:=&\hat{x}^{k}-\eta(\widetilde{G}y^{k}+v^{k+1})+\beta_{k}(\widetilde{G}y^{k-1}+v^{k}),\\
z^{k+1}&:=&z^{k}-\frac{\gamma_{k}}{s}(\widetilde{G}y^{k-1}+v^{k}),\end{array}\right.

(VFOG)

where
v^{k}\in Tx^{k}
,
t_{k}\geq s>1
,
\eta>0
,
\beta_{k}\geq 0
,
\gamma_{k}>0
are given parameters, determined later.
There are two key components of (
VFOG
) that require further discussion.

•

First, we will clearly characterize the conditions on the estimator
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
of
Gy^{k}
to guarantee the convergence of (
VFOG
).
We will also give concrete examples of
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
.

•

Second, we will specify the choice of parameters
t_{k}
,
s
,
\eta
,
\beta_{k}
, and
\gamma_{k}
in our convergence analysis to achieve better convergence than its non-accelerated counterparts.

The scheme (
VFOG
) is sufficiently general to cover the following special cases.

\mathrm{(a)}

If
\widetilde{G}y^{k}:=Gy^{k}
and
\widetilde{G}y^{k-1}:=Gx^{k}
, then we obtain a deterministic fast extragradient method for solving (
GE
).
It is similar to the one in
tran2025accelerated
;
yuan2024symplectic
, but the last step is different.

\mathrm{(b)}

If
\widetilde{G}y^{k}:=Gy^{k}
and
\widetilde{G}y^{k-1}:=Gy^{k-1}
, then we get a deterministic accelerated past-extragradient or fast optimistic gradient (OG) method for solving (
GE
).
This deterministic variant is still new under Assumptions
2
-
4
, and also different from
tran2025accelerated
.

Comparison with (
PEG
) and (
NAG
).

In fact, (
VFOG
) mimics Popov’s past-extragradient scheme, Nesterov’s acceleration, and variance reduction techniques to create such a new algorithm.
Specifically, suppose that
T=0
,
\widetilde{G}y^{k}=Gy^{k}
,
\beta_{k}=0
, and
s=0
, this scheme reduces to (
PEG
) discussed in Section
2
.
At the same time, it incorporates an intermediate step
\hat{x}^{k}=\frac{t_{k}-s}{t_{k}}x^{k}+\frac{s}{t_{k}}z^{k}
and uses a variable parameters
t_{k}
and
\beta_{k}
aka Nesterov’s accelerated method (
NAG
) in convex optimization.
However, the first fundamental difference between (
NAG
) and (
VFOG
) is the stepsize of
\frac{\eta t_{k}}{s}
vs.
\frac{\gamma_{k}}{s}
in the last line.
The second one is the search direction
\eta\widetilde{G}y^{k}+\beta_{k}\widetilde{G}y^{k-1}
in (
VFOG
) combining two consecutive approximations
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
and
\widetilde{G}y^{k-1}
of
G
, which acts like
\eta\nabla{f}(\hat{x}^{k})
in (
NAG
) for smooth and convex optimization.

The implementable version.

Since
v^{k+1}
is on the right-hand side of the third line of (
VFOG
), we can use the resolvent
J_{\eta T}
of
\eta T
to rewrite it equivalently to

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hat{x}^{k}&:=&\frac{s}{t_{k}}z^{k}+\frac{t_{k}-s}{t_{k}}x^{k},\\
y^{k}&:=&\hat{x}^{k}-(\eta-\beta_{k})(\widetilde{G}y^{k-1}+v^{k}),\\
x^{k+1}&:=&J_{\eta T}\big(\hat{x}^{k}-\eta\widetilde{G}y^{k}+\beta_{k}(\widetilde{G}y^{k-1}+v^{k})\big),\\
z^{k+1}&:=&z^{k}-\frac{\gamma_{k}}{s}(\widetilde{G}y^{k-1}+v^{k}),\\
v^{k+1}&:=&\frac{1}{\eta}\big(\hat{x}^{k}-x^{k+1}+\beta_{k}(\widetilde{G}y^{k-1}+v^{k})\big)-\widetilde{G}y^{k}.\end{array}\right.

(14)

Clearly, each iteration of (
14
) requires one evaluation of
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
and one evaluation of
J_{\eta T}
.

3.2
Main result 1: Convergence properties of
VFOG

There are different strategies to approximate
Gy^{k}
by
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
.
For instance, one can use increasing mini-batch stochastic approximation strategies as in
boct2021minibatch
;
pethick2023solving
or control variate techniques as in
Defazio2014
;
SVRG
;
nguyen2017sarah
.
In what follows, we do not specify how to approximate
Gy^{k}
by
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
, but introduce a general recursive bound of the error term
\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\|\widetilde{G}y^{k}-Gy^{k}\|^{2}\big]
.

Approximation condition for
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
.

We assume that
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
is constructed by accessing the unbiased stochastic oracle
\mathbf{G}_{\xi}y^{k}
of
Gy^{k}
adapted to the filtration
\{\mathcal{F}_{k}\}
such that for
k\geq 1
, there exist two constants
\kappa\in(0,1]
and
\Theta\geq 0
, a nonnegative sequence
\{\delta_{k}\}
, and a sequence of nonnegative random variables
\{\Delta_{k}\}
such that the following condition holds almost surely for the error term
e^{k}:=\widetilde{G}y^{k}-Gy^{k}
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\|e^{k}\|^{2}\big]&\leq&\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\Delta_{k}\big],\\
\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\Delta_{k}\big]&\leq&(1-\kappa)\Delta_{k-1}+\Theta L^{2}\|x^{k}-y^{k-1}\|^{2}+\frac{\delta_{k}}{t_{k}^{2}},\end{array}\right.

(15)

where
\{(x^{k},y^{k})\}
and
\{t_{k}\}
are from (
VFOG
).

The condition (
15
) is rather general.
Indeed, if
\kappa=1
and
\Theta=0
, then it reduces to
\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\|e^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\frac{\delta_{k}}{t_{k}^{2}}
.
If
\kappa=1
and
\delta_{k}=0
, then it becomes
\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\|e^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\Theta L^{2}\|x^{k}-y^{k-1}\|^{2}
, which can be viewed as an adaptive error bound condition.
One way to choose
\delta_{k}
is
\delta_{k}:=\frac{\delta}{(k+r)^{1+\nu}}
for any
\nu>0
and a given
\delta>0
.
In the sequel, we will denote by
\mathcal{S}_{K}:=\sum_{k=0}^{K}\delta_{k}
.

Adaptive mini-batch estimator.

One way to construct
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
is using a mini-batch estimator with increasing size.
Given a mini-batch
\mathcal{B}_{k}
of size
b_{k}
, we construct

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\widetilde{G}y^{k}&:=&\frac{1}{b_{k}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{B}_{k}}\mathbf{G}_{\xi}y^{k}.\end{array}

(16)

It is well-known that
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}\big[\|\widetilde{G}y^{k}-Gy^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\frac{\sigma^{2}}{b_{k}}
, where
\sigma^{2}
is the variance of the stochastic oracle
\mathbf{G}_{\xi}(\cdot)
in Assumption
1
.
To guarantee the condition (
15
), we need to choose
b_{k}
adaptively.
For example, we can choose
b_{k}
using the following condition:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\frac{\sigma^{2}}{b_{k}}\leq\frac{(1-\kappa)\sigma^{2}}{b_{k-1}}+\Theta L^{2}\|x^{k}-y^{k-1}\|^{2}+\frac{\delta_{k}}{t_{k}^{2}}.\end{array}

(17)

Clearly, by setting
\Delta_{k}:=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{b_{k}}
,
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
defined by (
16
) satisfies (
15
).
Moreover, the condition (
17
) leads to an increasing mini-batch size
b_{k}
as used in several previous works such as
boct2021minibatch
;
pethick2023solving
.
Nevertheless, (
15
) can cover a wide range of estimators, including unbiased and biased instances, beyond the classical mini-batch instance (
16
).

Convergence guarantees.

Given
s\geq 2
,
\kappa
and
\Theta
in (
15
), we first define

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\Lambda_{0}&:=&\frac{s^{2}(16s-18)}{(s-2)[s^{2}-(1-\kappa)(s+1)^{2}]}\\
\omega&:=&1+\frac{2(11s-13)}{s-2}+\frac{2\Lambda_{0}\Theta(s+1)^{2}}{s^{2}},\\
\mu&:=&\frac{3s-2}{8(s-1)\sqrt{2(1+\omega)(s+1)}},\\
\lambda&:=&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2(1+\omega)(s+1)}}.\end{array}

(18)

It is easy to check that these constants are positive and
\lambda\in(0,1)
.

Now, let us state the convergence of (
VFOG
) in Theorem
3.1
below for the recursive error bound condition (
15
).
The proof of Theorem
3.1
is given in Subsection
5.1
.

Theorem 3.1

Let
\Lambda_{0}
,
\mu
, and
\lambda
be defined by (
18
).
For (
GE
), suppose that
G
satisfies Assumption
1
and Assumption
2
with
\alpha=1

(
i.e.,
G
is
L
-Lipschitz continuous
)
, and
\Phi
satisfies Assumption
4
with
\rho_{n}\geq 0
and
\rho_{c}=0

(
i.e.,
\Phi
is
\rho_{n}
-co-hypomonotone
)
such that
L\rho_{n}\leq\mu
.

Let
\{(x^{k},y^{k},z^{k})\}
be generated by (
VFOG
) using an estimator
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
of
Gy^{k}
satisfying the condition (
15
) with
\frac{2s+1}{(s+1)^{2}}<\kappa\leq 1
for a given
s>7

and the following parameters:

\hskip-2.15277pt\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{8(s-1)\rho_{n}}{3s-2}\leq\eta<\frac{\lambda}{L},\quad\gamma_{k}:=\frac{\eta(k+s)}{(s-2)(k+s+1)},\quad\textrm{and}\quad\beta_{k}:=\big[\frac{(s-2)\eta}{4(s-1)}+2\rho_{n}]\frac{k+1}{k+s+1}-\frac{\gamma_{k}}{k+s+1}.\end{array}\hskip 0.0pt

(19)

Then, the following statement holds:

\mathrm{(a)}

(
Summability bounds
)

There exist
v^{k}\in Tx^{k}
for all
k\geq 0
such that

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\sum_{k=0}^{K}\big[(s^{2}-4s-2)\eta-8(s-1)\rho_{n}\big](k+1)\mathbb{E}\left[\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}\right]&\leq&8\eta\big(\mathcal{R}_{0}^{2}+\Lambda_{0}\mathcal{S}_{K}\big),\\
\sum_{k=0}^{K}s(k+s+1)\mathbb{E}\left[\|Gy^{k-1}+v^{k}\|^{2}\right]&\leq&2\mathcal{R}_{0}^{2}+2\Lambda_{0}\mathcal{S}_{K},\end{array}

(20)

where
\mathcal{S}_{K}:=\sum_{k=0}^{K}\delta_{k}
and
\mathcal{R}_{0}^{2}
is given by

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{R}_{0}^{2}&:=&\frac{(3s-2)(s-2)s^{2}-4(s-1)^{2}}{8(s-1)}\|Gx^{0}+v^{0}\|^{2}+\frac{s(s^{2}-1)(s-2)}{2\eta^{2}}\|x^{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}.\end{array}

(21)

Here, the second line of (
20
) requires
s>2+\sqrt{6}\approx 4.4495
and
\eta>\frac{8(s-1)\rho_{n}}{s^{2}-4s-2}
.

\mathrm{(b)}

(
The
\mathcal{O}\left(1/k^{2}\right)
convergence rates
)
We have the following bounds:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]&\leq&\dfrac{C_{0}(\mathcal{R}_{0}^{2}+\Lambda_{0}\mathcal{S}_{k})}{(k+s)^{2}}\\
\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gy^{k-1}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]&\leq&\dfrac{2C_{0}(\mathcal{R}_{0}^{2}+\Lambda_{0}\mathcal{S}_{k})}{(k+s)^{2}},\end{array}

(22)

where
C_{0}:=\frac{16(s-1)(s-2)\eta}{(3s^{2}-8s-1)\eta-8(s-1)(s-2)\rho_{n}}
.

\mathrm{(c)}

(
The
o\left(1/k^{2}\right)
-convergence rates
)

If additionally
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}:=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\delta_{k}<+\infty
, then

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\lim_{k\to\infty}k^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]=0\ \quad\textrm{and}\ \quad\lim_{k\to\infty}k^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gy^{k-1}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]=0.\end{array}

(23)

\mathrm{(d)}

(
Iteration-complexity
)

For a given
\epsilon>0
, the total number of iterations
k
to achieve
\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\epsilon^{2}
is at most
\mathcal{O}\big(1/\epsilon\big)
.
This is also the number of evaluations of
J_{\eta T}
.

Next, we state the almost sure convergence rates and the almost sure convergence of
\{x^{k}\}
and
\{y^{k}\}
in the following theorem, whose proof is deferred to Subsection
5.2
.

Theorem 3.2

Under the same assumptions and settings as in Theorem
3.1
, assuming that
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}:=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\delta_{k}<+\infty
, we have the following statements.

\mathrm{(a)}

The following summability results hold:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(k+1)\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}&<&+\infty,\quad\textrm{almost surely},\\
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(k+s)\|Gy^{k-1}+v^{k}\|^{2}&<&+\infty,\quad\textrm{almost surely}.\end{array}

(24)

\mathrm{(b)}

The following limits hold almost surely:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\lim_{k\to\infty}k^{2}\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}=0\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lim_{k\to\infty}k^{2}\|Gy^{k-1}+v^{k}\|^{2}=0.\end{array}

(25)

\mathrm{(c)}

If, additionally,
\mathrm{gra}(\Phi)
is closed, then
\{x^{k}\}
almost surely converges to a
\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)
-valued random variable
x^{\star}\in\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)
.
In addition, the other two sequences of iterates
\{y^{k}\}
and
\{z^{k}\}
also almost surely converge to the same random variable
x^{\star}\in\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)
.

Note that if Assumption
4
holds with
\rho_{c}=0
, then Assumption
3
also automatically holds with
\rho_{*}=\rho_{n}
.
Therefore, we do not require Assumption
3
in Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
.

If
T
is
\rho_{T}
-co-hypomonotone for some
\rho_{T}\geq 0
, then, as proven in
TranDinh2025a
, the resolvent
J_{\lambda T}
is well-defined, single-valued, and nonexpansive if
\lambda>\rho_{T}
.
Let us define the following forward-backward splitting residual of (
GE
), widely used in monotone operators:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{G}_{\lambda}x:=\frac{1}{\lambda}\big(x-J_{\lambda T}(x-\lambda Gx)\big).\end{array}

It is well-known that
x^{\star}\in\mathrm{zer}(\Phi)
iff
\mathcal{G}_{\lambda}x^{\star}=0
.
Therefore, for a given tolerance
\epsilon>0
, we can say that
x^{k}
is an
\epsilon
-solution of (
GE
) (in expectation) if
\mathbb{E}\big[\|\mathcal{G}_{\lambda}x^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\epsilon^{2}
.
Since
\|\mathcal{G}_{\lambda}x^{k}\|\leq\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|
for any
v^{k}\in Tx^{k}
(see
tran2024revisiting
), if we obtain
\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\epsilon^{2}
as in Theorem
3.1
, then we conclude that
x^{k}
is also an
\epsilon
-solution of (
GE
), i.e.,
\mathbb{E}\big[\|\mathcal{G}_{\lambda}x^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\epsilon^{2}
.

Remark 1

Note that if
\Phi
is maximally monotone, then
\mathrm{gra}(\Phi)
is closed, see
(
Bauschke2011
, Proposition 20.38)
.
However, the closedness of
\mathrm{gra}(\Phi)
is weaker than the maximal monotonicity of
\Phi
.
For instance, if
\Phi
is upper semi-continuous and has closed values, then
\mathrm{gra}(\Phi)
is closed.
In the single-valued case, one can replace this closedness assumption of
\mathrm{gra}(\Phi)
by a demiclosedness of
\Phi
, see, e.g.,
davis2022variance
.
If
T=0
, then
\Phi=G
, which is continuous, and thus
\mathrm{gra}(G)
is closed.

3.3
Oracle complexity of
VFOG
with adaptive mini-batching estimators

Now, we estimate the oracle complexity of (
VFOG
) using the adaptive mini-batch estimator
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
in (
16
) in the following corollary, whose proof can be found in Appendix
C.5
.

Corollary 1

Under the same conditions and settings as in Theorem
3.1
, if
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
constructed by (
16
) with increasing mini-batch size
b_{k}:=\big\lfloor\frac{\sigma^{2}(k+s)^{3+\nu}}{\delta}\big\rfloor
for some constants
\nu>0
and
\delta>0
is used in (
VFOG
), then the expected total number of oracle calls
\mathbf{G}_{\xi}(\cdot)
to achieve
x^{K}
such that
\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{K}+\xi^{K}\|^{2}\big]\leq\epsilon^{2}
for a given tolerance
\epsilon>0
and
\xi^{K}\in Tx^{K}
is at most
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{K}\big]=\mathcal{O}\big(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\epsilon^{4+\nu}}\big)
.
The expected total number of
J_{\eta T}
evaluations is at most
\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-1}\big)
.

Alternatively, if we choose
b_{k}:=\big\lfloor\frac{\sigma^{2}(k+s)^{3}\log(k+s)}{\delta}\big\rfloor
, then the above complexity can be slightly improved to
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{K}\big]=\mathcal{O}\big(\frac{\sigma^{2}\log(\epsilon^{-1})}{\epsilon^{4}}\big)
.
In this case, the expected total number of
J_{\eta T}
evaluations is at most
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-1})
.

The work
boct2021minibatch
studied VIPs, a special case of (
GE
), and established a
\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-4}\ln(\epsilon^{-1})\big)
oracle complexity using increasing mini-batch estimators as (
16
).
Our complexity in Corollary
1
is either
\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-4+\nu}\big)
for any sufficiently small
\nu>0
or
\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon^{-4}\ln(\ln(\epsilon^{-1}))\big)
depending on the choice of
b_{k}
, which is comparable with
boct2021minibatch
.
Nevertheless, our assumptions, Assumptions
3
and
4
, are different from the pseudo-monotonicity in
boct2021minibatch
.
Moreover, as mentioned earlier, our method can work with any unbiased and biased estimators under the condition (
15
), beyond (
16
).
Increasing mini-batches were also used in
pethick2023solving
, but the authors did not explicitly derive any oracle complexity, and also used different assumptions and metric.

Note that our mini-batch size
b_{k}
in Corollary
1
is loosely chosen from the condition (
15
) with
\kappa=1
and
\Theta=0
.
However, we can directly use the condition (
17
) to estimate
b_{k}
, which gives us a smaller batch-size than the worst case
b_{k}
chosen in Corollary
1
.

Corollary
1
also shows that if we use standard stochastic approximation (
16
) for
Gy^{k}
, then our accelerated method (
VFOG
) does not achieve better complexity than
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-4})
.
This is due to the accumulation of errors in accelerated methods compared to non-accelerated ones as studied in
Devolder2010
.
To achieve better complexity bounds, other variance-reduction techniques should be exploited.
For example, in Section
4
below, we will use control variate techniques.

4
Convergence and Complexity of
VFOG
using Control Variate Estimators

In this section, we will employ control variate techniques to develop a variance-reduction variant of (
VFOG
) to solve (
GE
).
Unlike Section
3
, in this section, we let
\alpha\in[0,1)
in Assumption
2
and require
\rho_{c}>0
in Assumption
4
.

4.1
A class of stochastic variance-reduced estimators

We propose to use the following class of variance-reduced estimators
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
of
Gy^{k}
, which was introduced in our recent work
TranDinh2025a
.
This class of estimators is relatively broad and covers various well-known instances, including loopless SVRG, SAGA, and loopless SARAH.

Definition 2

Given
\underline{\kappa}\in(0,1)
and
\Theta>0
, let
\{y^{k}\}
be the sequence of iterates generated by (
VFOG
) and
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
be a stochastic estimator of
Gy^{k}
constructed from an i.i.d. sample
\mathcal{B}_{k}\subseteq[n]
, adapted to the filtration
\{\mathcal{F}_{k}\}
.
We say that
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
satisfies a
\textbf{VR}(\Delta_{k};\kappa_{k},\Theta_{k},\delta_{k})

(
variance-reduction
)
property if there exist two parameters
\kappa_{k}\in[\underline{\kappa},1)
and
\Theta_{k}\in[\underline{\Theta},\bar{\Theta}]
, a nonnegative sequence
\left\{\delta_{k}\right\}
, and a nonnegative random quantity
\Delta_{k}
such that
(
almost surely
)
:

{\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\|\widetilde{G}y^{k}-Gy^{k}\|^{2}\big]&\leq&\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\Delta_{k}\big],\\
\mathbb{E}_{k}\big[\Delta_{k}\big]&\leq&(1-\kappa_{k})\Delta_{k-1}+\Theta_{k}\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\big[\|\mathbf{G}_{\xi}y^{k}-\mathbf{G}_{\xi}y^{k-1}\|^{2}\big]+\frac{\delta_{k}}{t_{k}^{2}},\end{array}\right.}\vskip-2.15277pt

(26)

where
\{y^{k}\}
and
\{t_{k}\}
are from (
VFOG
).

Definition
2
covers both unbiased and biased estimators
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
of
Gy^{k}
since we do not impose the unbiased condition
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}\big[\widetilde{G}y^{k}\big]=Gy^{k}
.
It is also different from existing works, including
driggs2019accelerating
;
tran2024accelerated
;
TranDinh2024
, as we only require the condition (
26
), making it more broader to cover several existing estimators that may violate the definitions in
driggs2019accelerating
;
tran2024accelerated
;
TranDinh2024
.
Our class is also different from other generalizations such as
beznosikov2023stochastic
;
gorbunov2022stochastic
;
loizou2021stochastic
for stochastic optimization since they required the unbiasedness and additional or different conditions.

Now we provide three examples: loopless-SVRG, SAGA, and loopless-SARAH, that satisfy Definition
2
.
Note that the loopless-SVRG and SAGA are unbiased, while the loopless-SARAH is biased.
In this paper, we only focus on these three estimators, but we believe that other variance-reduced estimators such as Hybrid SGD
Tran-Dinh2019
;
Tran-Dinh2019a
, SAG
LeRoux2012
;
schmidt2017minimizing
, SARGE
driggs2019bias
, SEGA
hanzely2018sega
, and JacSketch
gower2021stochastic
can possibly be used in our methods.

4.1.1
Example 1: L-SVRG estimator

SVRG was introduced in
SVRG
, and its loopless version, called L-SVRG, was proposed in
kovalev2019don
.
We show that this estimator satisfies Definition
2
.

For given
G
in (
GE
), two iterates
y^{k}
and
\bar{y}^{k}
, and i.i.d. sample
\mathcal{B}_{k}
of size
b_{k}
, we define
\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}(\cdot):=\frac{1}{b_{k}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{B}_{k}}\mathbf{G}_{\xi}(\cdot)
is a mini-batch estimator, and then construct
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
as follows:

{\widetilde{G}y^{k}:=G\bar{y}^{k}+\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}y^{k}-\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}\bar{y}^{k},}\vskip-2.15277pt

(L-SVRG)

where, for all
k\geq 1
,
\bar{y}^{k}
is updated by

{\bar{y}^{k}:=\begin{cases}y^{k-1}&\text{with probability}~\mathbf{p}_{k}\\
\bar{y}^{k-1}&\text{with probability}~1-\mathbf{p}_{k}.\end{cases}}\vskip-2.15277pt

(27)

Here,
\mathbf{p}_{k}\in(0,1)
is a given Bernoulli’s probability distribution and
\bar{y}^{0}:=y^{0}
.

The following lemma shows that
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
satisfies Definition
2
, whose proof is given in
TranDinh2025a
.

Lemma 1

The estimator
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
constructed by (
L-SVRG
) such that
b_{k}\geq b_{k-1}
satisfies Definition
2
with

\kappa_{k}:=\frac{\mathbf{p}_{k}}{2}
,
\Theta_{k}:=\frac{4}{b_{k}\mathbf{p}_{k}}
,
\delta_{k}=0
, and
\Delta_{k}:=\frac{1}{b_{k}}\mathbb{E}_{\xi}\big[\|\mathbf{G}_{\xi}y^{k}-\mathbf{G}_{\xi}\bar{y}^{k}\|^{2}\big]
.

4.1.2
Example 2: SAGA estimator

SAGA was introduced in
Defazio2014
for solving finite-sum convex optimization.
We apply it to (
GE
) when
\xi
has a finite support
\xi\in\Omega:=\{\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{n}\}
, which is constructed as follows.

For given
\mathbf{G}_{\xi}
in (
GE
) with a finite support
\Omega:=\{\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{n}\}
, iterate sequence
\{y^{k}\}_{k\geq 0}
, and i.i.d. sample
\mathcal{B}_{k}\subseteq[n]
of size
b_{k}
, for
k\geq 1
, we update
\widehat{\mathbf{G}}_{\xi}y^{k}
for all
\xi\in\Omega
as follows:

{\widehat{\mathbf{G}}_{\xi}y^{k}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathbf{G}_{\xi}y^{k-1}&\text{if}~\xi\in\mathcal{B}_{k},\\
\widehat{\mathbf{G}}_{\xi}y^{k-1}&\text{if}~\xi\notin\mathcal{B}_{k}\quad\textrm{(no update)}.\end{array}\right.}\vskip-2.15277pt

(28)

Then, we construct a SAGA estimator
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
for
Gy^{k}
as follows:

{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\widetilde{G}y^{k}&:=&\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\xi\in\Omega}\widehat{\mathbf{G}}_{\xi}y^{k}+\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}y^{k}-\widehat{\mathbf{G}}_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}y^{k},\end{array}}\vskip-2.15277pt

(SAGA)

where
\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}y^{k}:=\frac{1}{b_{k}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{B}_{k}}\mathbf{G}_{\xi}y^{k}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{G}}_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}y^{k}:=\frac{1}{b_{k}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{B}_{k}}\widehat{\mathbf{G}}_{\xi}y^{k}
.
In this case, we need to store
n
component
\widehat{\mathbf{G}}^{k}_{\xi}
computed so far for all
\xi\in\Omega
in a table
\mathbf{T}_{k}:=[\widehat{\mathbf{G}}_{\xi_{1}}y^{k},\cdots,\widehat{\mathbf{G}}_{\xi_{n}}y^{k}]
initialized at
\widehat{\mathbf{G}}_{\xi}y^{0}:=\mathbf{G}_{\xi}y^{0}
for all
\xi\in\Omega
.
SAGA requires significant memory to store
\mathbf{T}_{k}
if
n
and
p
are both large.
We have the following result, whose proof can be found in
TranDinh2025a
.

Lemma 2

The estimator
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
constructed by (
SAGA
) such that
b_{k-1}-\frac{b_{k}b_{k-1}}{4n}\leq b_{k}\leq b_{k-1}
satisfies Definition
2
with
\kappa_{k}:=\frac{b_{k}}{2n}
,
\Theta_{k}:=\frac{5n}{b_{k}^{2}}
,
\delta_{k}=0
, and
\Delta_{k}:=\frac{1}{nb_{k}}{\displaystyle\sum_{\xi\in\Omega}}\|\mathbf{G}_{\xi}y^{k}-\widehat{\mathbf{G}}_{\xi}y^{k}\|^{2}
.

4.1.3
Example 3: L-SARAH estimator

SARAH was introduced in
nguyen2017sarah
for finite-sum convex optimization.
Its loopless variant, called L-SARAH, was studied in
driggs2019accelerating
;
li2020page
for convex and nonconvex optimization, and recently in
cai2023variance
;
cai2022stochastic
;
TranDinh2025a
for co-coercive root-finding problems.
This estimator is constructed as follows.

For given
G
in (
GE
), iterate sequence
\{y^{k}\}
, and i.i.d. sample
\mathcal{B}_{k}\subseteq[n]
of size
b_{k}
, we construct
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
as

{\widetilde{G}y^{k}:=\begin{cases}\widetilde{G}y^{k-1}+\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}y^{k}-\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}y^{k-1}&\text{with probability}~1-\mathbf{p}_{k},\\
Gy^{k}&\text{with probability}~\mathbf{p}_{k},\end{cases}}\vskip-2.15277pt

(L-SARAH)

where
\mathbf{G}_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}(\cdot):=\frac{1}{b_{k}}\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{B}_{k}}\mathbf{G}_{\xi}(\cdot)
,
\widetilde{G}y^{0}:=Gy^{0}
, and
\mathbf{p}_{k}\in[\underline{\mathbf{p}},1)
is a given switching Bernoulli’s probability distribution and
\underline{\mathbf{p}}\in(0,1)
is a given lower bound.
The following lemma shows that
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
satisfies Definition
2
, whose proof is presented in
TranDinh2025a
.

Lemma 3

The estimator
\widetilde{F}_{k}
constructed by (
L-SARAH
) satisfies Definition
2
with

\kappa_{k}:=\mathbf{p}_{k}
,
\Theta_{k}:=\frac{1}{b_{k}}
,
\delta_{k}=0
, and
\Delta_{k}:=\|\widetilde{G}y^{k}-Gy^{k}\|^{2}
.

4.2
Main result 2: Convergence properties of
VFOG
using
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
in Definition
2

For given
s>2
,
\bar{c}
in (
74
), and
L
and
\alpha\in[0,1)
in Assumption
3
, we define

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hat{\omega}&:=&\frac{3s-2}{2(1-\alpha)(s-1)}+\frac{2(11s-12)}{s-2},\\
\hat{\lambda}&:=&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2(s+1)(1+\hat{\omega})}},\\
\hat{\mu}&:=&\frac{\hat{\lambda}(3s-2)}{8(s-1)},\\
\Gamma&:=&\frac{3s^{2}(16s-19)}{(s-2)(s+1)},\\
\hat{\Lambda}_{0}&:=&\frac{(16s-19)}{\bar{c}(s-2)}.\end{array}

(29)

It is easy to check that these constants are positive and
\hat{\lambda}\in(0,1)
.

Now, we are ready to state the convergence and convergence rates of (
VFOG
), whose proof is deferred to Subsection
5.3
for the sake of presentation.

Theorem 4.1

Let
\hat{\mu}
,
\hat{\lambda}
, and
\Gamma
be defined by (
29
).
Suppose that
G
and
T
in (
GE
) satisfy Assumptions
1
,
2
,
3
, and
4
with
\alpha\in[0,1)
,
\rho_{n}\geq\rho_{c}>0
,
0\leq\rho_{*}\leq\rho_{n}
, and
L\rho_{n}\leq\hat{\mu}
.

Let
\{(x^{k},y^{k},z^{k})\}
be generated by (
VFOG
) using a variance-reduced estimator
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
of
Gy^{k}
satisfying Definition
2
and
\eta
,
\gamma_{k}
, and
\beta_{k}
such that:

\hskip-1.07639pt\begin{array}[]{lcl}\frac{8(s-1)\rho_{n}}{3s-2}\leq\eta<\frac{\hat{\lambda}}{L},\quad\gamma_{k}:=\frac{\eta(k+s)}{(s-2)(k+s+1)},\quad\textrm{and}\quad\beta_{k}:=\big[\frac{(s-2)\eta}{4(s-1)}+2\rho_{n}]\frac{k+1}{k+s+1}-\frac{\gamma_{k}}{k+s+1}.\end{array}\hskip-1.07639pt

(30)

Suppose further that both
\kappa_{k}
and
\Theta_{k}
in Definition
2
satisfy the following condition:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\kappa_{k}\geq\frac{\eta\Gamma}{\rho_{c}}\Theta_{k}+\frac{2}{k+s+1}.\end{array}

(31)

Then, the following statements hold:

\mathrm{(a)}

(
The
\mathcal{O}\left(1/k^{2}\right)
convergence rates
)
We have the following bounds:

\hskip-12.91663pt\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\dfrac{\hat{C}_{0}(\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{2}+\hat{\Lambda_{0}}\mathcal{S}_{k})}{(k+s)^{2}}\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gy^{k-1}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\dfrac{2\hat{C}_{0}(\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{2}+\hat{\Lambda_{0}}\mathcal{S}_{k})}{(k+s)^{2}},\end{array}\hskip-12.91663pt

(32)

where
v^{k}\in Tx^{k}
,
\mathcal{S}_{k}:=\sum_{l=0}^{k}\delta_{l}
, and
\hat{C}_{0}
and
\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{2}
are respectively given by

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\hat{C}_{0}&:=&\frac{16(s-1)(s-2)\eta}{(3s^{2}-8s-1)\eta-8(s-1)(s-2)\rho_{n}},\\
\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{2}&:=&\frac{(3s-2)(s-2)s^{2}-4(s-1)^{2}}{8(s-1)}\|Gx^{0}+v^{0}\|^{2}+\frac{s(s^{2}-1)(s-2)}{2\eta^{2}}\|x^{0}-x^{\star}\|^{2}.\end{array}

(33)

\mathrm{(b)}

(
Summability bounds
)

The following bounds also hold:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\sum_{k=0}^{K}s(k+s+1)\mathbb{E}\left[\|Gy^{k-1}+v^{k}\|^{2}\right]&\leq&2(\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{2}+\hat{\Lambda}_{0}\mathcal{S}_{K}),\\
\sum_{k=0}^{K}\frac{[(s^{2}-4s-2)\eta-8(s-1)(s-2)\rho_{n}]}{(s-2)}(k+1)\mathbb{E}\left[\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}\right]&\leq&8\eta(\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{2}+\hat{\Lambda}_{0}\mathcal{S}_{K}),\end{array}

(34)

where the second line requires
s>2+\sqrt{6}
and
(s^{2}-4s-2)\eta>8(s-1)(s-2)\rho_{n}
.

\mathrm{(c)}

(
The
o\left(1/k^{2}\right)
-convergence rates
)

If, in addition,
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}:=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\delta_{k}<+\infty
, then we also have the following limits:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\lim_{k\to\infty}k^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]=0\quad\textrm{and}\quad\lim_{k\to\infty}k^{2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gy^{k-1}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]=0.\end{array}

(35)

\mathrm{(d)}

(
Iteration-complexity
)

For a given
\epsilon>0
, the total number of iterations
k
to achieve
\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\epsilon^{2}
is at most
\mathcal{O}\big(1/\epsilon\big)
.
This is also the number of evaluations of
J_{\eta T}
.

\mathrm{(e)}

(
Almost sure convergence
)

The conclusions of Theorem
3.2
still hold in this case.

The constant
s
in Theorem
4.1
was left free.
It gives us a freedom to flexibly choose its value for a concrete implementation.
For example, if we choose
s=5
and assume that
\alpha=0
in (
3
), then from (
30
), we get
\hat{\omega}=29.625
.
Consequently, we have
\hat{\lambda}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{367.5}}\approx 0.052164
and
\hat{\mu}:=\frac{\hat{\lambda}(3s-2)}{8(s-1)}\approx 0.021192
.
In this case, our conditions
L\rho_{n}\leq\hat{\mu}
and
\frac{16\rho_{n}}{7}\leq\eta\leq\frac{\hat{\lambda}}{L}
in Theorem
4.1
hold.
Note that the bounds of these parameters are not tight, and their range can be substantially improved by carefully refining our analysis.

Theorem
4.1
only shows convergence rates of (
VFOG
), but not its oracle complexity.
It also requires the condition (
31
) to hold for
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
in Definition
2
.
The iteration-complexity of (
VFOG
) for achieving an
\epsilon
-solution
x^{k}
in expectation, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{k}+v^{k}\|^{2}\big]\leq\epsilon^{2}
is
\mathcal{O}\big(1/\epsilon\big)
, improving by a factor
\frac{1}{\epsilon}
over its non-accelerated counterparts, including
TranDinh2024
.

4.3
Oracle complexity of (
VFOG
) using L-SVRG, SAGA, and L-SARAH

In this subsection, we specify the oracle complexity of (
VFOG
) using three concrete estimators: L-SVRG, SAGA, and L-SARAH in Subsection
4.1
.
The key point is to construct a distribution
\mathbf{p}_{k}
and choose an appropriate mini-batch size
b_{k}
to fulfill the condition (
31
).
Note that from Lemmas
1
,
2
, and
3
, since
\delta_{k}=0
, we always have
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}:=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\delta_{k}=0
for these three estimators.
Now, let us start with the loopless SVRG estimator in (
L-SVRG
).

Corollary 2

Suppose that Assumptions
2
-
4
hold for (
GE
) under the finite-sum setting (
2
).
Let
\{(x^{k},y^{k},z^{k})\}
be generated by (
VFOG
) using the SVRG estimator
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
from (
L-SVRG
) for
Gy^{k}
and the parameters as in Theorem
4.1
.
For given
\nu\in(0,1/2)
and
c_{2}>0
, if
n\geq\max\big\{[2c_{2}]^{1/\nu},\ \big[\frac{8\Gamma\eta}{c_{2}^{2}\rho_{c}}\big]^{1/(1-2\nu)}\big\}
for
\Gamma
in (
29
), and
b_{k}
and
\mathbf{p}_{k}
in (
27
) are chosen such that

\begin{array}[]{lcl}b_{k}=b:=\big\lfloor\frac{8\Gamma\eta n^{2\nu}}{c_{2}^{2}\rho_{c}}\big\rfloor\quad\textrm{and}\quad\mathbf{p}_{k}:=\begin{cases}\frac{c_{2}}{n^{\nu}}+\frac{4}{k+s+1}&\textrm{if}~k\leq\lfloor\frac{4n^{\nu}}{c_{2}}-s\rfloor,\\
\frac{2c_{2}}{n^{\nu}}&\textrm{otherwise}.\end{cases}\end{array}

(36)

Then, we have
1\leq b_{k}\leq n
and
\mathbf{p}_{k}\in(0,1]
.
For any
\epsilon>0
, the expected total number of oracle calls
\mathbf{G}_{\xi}y^{k}
for
k\geq 0
in (
VFOG
) is at most

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{K}\big]&=&\mathcal{O}\left(n\ln(n^{\nu})+n^{3\nu}+\frac{\mathcal{R}_{0}(n^{1-\nu}+n^{2\nu})}{\epsilon}\right)\end{array}

(37)

to achieve
\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{K}+v^{K}\|^{2}\big]\leq\epsilon^{2}
.
If we choose
\nu=\frac{1}{3}
, then
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{t}\big]=\mathcal{O}\big(n\ln(n^{1/3})+\frac{n^{2/3}}{\epsilon}\big)
.

Proof

Since
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
is updated by (
L-SVRG
),
by Lemma
1
, we have
\kappa_{k}=\frac{\mathbf{p}_{k}}{2}
and
\Theta_{k}=\frac{4}{b_{k}\mathbf{p}_{k}}
.
For
\Gamma:=\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}3s^{2}(16s-18)}}{(s-2)(s+1)}
from (
29
), since
\kappa_{k}=\frac{\mathbf{p}_{k}}{2}
and
\Theta_{k}=\frac{4}{b_{k}\mathbf{p}_{k}}
, the condition (
31
) holds if

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbf{p}_{k}&\geq&\frac{8\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}b_{k}\mathbf{p}_{k}}+\frac{4}{k+s+1}.\end{array}

Let
\nu\in[0,1]
,
c_{1}>0
, and
c_{2}>0
be given.
If we choose
\mathbf{p}_{k}\geq\frac{c_{2}}{n^{\nu}}
and
b_{k}\geq c_{1}n^{2\nu}
, then the last condition holds if

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbf{p}_{k}&\geq&\frac{8\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{1}c_{2}n^{\nu}}}+\frac{4}{k+s+1}\geq\frac{8\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{1}c_{2}n^{\nu}}}=:\underline{\mathbf{p}}>0.\end{array}

(38)

Therefore, if we choose
c_{1}=\frac{8\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{2}^{2}}}
, then
\frac{8\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{1}c_{2}n^{\nu}}}=\frac{c_{2}}{n^{\nu}}
.
Moreover, if
k\geq\frac{4n^{\nu}}{c_{2}}-s
, then
\frac{4}{k+s+1}\leq\frac{c_{2}}{n^{\nu}}
.
Overall, if we choose
\mathbf{p}_{k}
as in (
36
), then it satisfies (
38
).

On the other hand, to guarantee
\mathbf{p}_{k}\leq 1
, we require
\frac{2c_{2}}{n^{\nu}}\leq 1
, which is guaranteed if
n\geq[2c_{2}]^{1/\nu}
.
In addition, we need
\frac{8\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{2}^{2}}}n^{2\nu}=c_{1}n^{2\nu}\leq b\leq n
, which holds if
n\geq\big[\frac{8\Gamma}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{2}^{2}}}\big]^{1/(1-2\nu)}
.
Combining these two conditions, we get
n\geq\max\big\{[2c_{2}]^{1/\nu},\ \big[\frac{8\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{2}^{2}}}\big]^{1/(1-2\nu)}\big\}
.
In this case, we can choose
b:=\big\lfloor\frac{8\Gamma\eta n^{2\nu}}{\rho_{c}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{2}^{2}}}\big\rfloor
as in (
36
).
We can also easily verify that
b\geq 1
.

Now, from (
32
) of Theorem
4.1
and
S_{K}=0
, to guarantee
\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{K}+v^{K}\|^{2}\big]\leq\epsilon^{2}
, we impose
\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{C}_{0}^{2}\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}^{2}}}{(K+s)^{2}}\leq\epsilon^{2}
for
\hat{C}_{0}^{2}:={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{16(s-1)(s-2)\eta}{(3s^{2}-8s-1)\eta-8(s-1)(s-2)\rho_{n}}}
.
This leads to the choice
K:=\big\lfloor\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{C}_{0}\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}}}{\epsilon}\big\rfloor
.

The expected total evaluations of
\mathbf{G}_{\xi}y^{k}
is
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{K}\big]=n+\mathbb{E}\big[\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{K}\big]
, where
n
is the total evaluations of the first epoch (
k=0
), and
\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{K}
is the evaluations of the next
K
iterations.
Using (
36
), we can evaluate the expected total number
\mathbb{E}\big[\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{K}\big]
of oracle calls
\mathbf{G}_{\xi}y^{k}
as follows:

{\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}\big[\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{K}\big]&:=&\sum_{k=1}^{K}[n\mathbf{p}_{k}+2(1-\mathbf{p}_{k})b]\leq\sum_{k=1}^{K}(n\mathbf{p}_{k}+2b)\\
&\leq&\sum_{k=1}^{\frac{4n^{\nu}}{c_{2}}-s}\big(c_{2}n^{1-\nu}+\frac{4n}{k+s+1}+2c_{1}n^{2\nu}\big)+\big(K-\frac{4n^{\nu}}{c_{2}}+s)(2c_{2}n^{1-\nu}+2c_{1}n^{2\nu})\\
&\leq&4n+4n\ln\big(\frac{4n^{\nu}}{c_{2}}+2\big)+c_{1}n^{3\nu}+\frac{(2c_{2}n^{1-\nu}+2c_{1}n^{2\nu}){\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{C}_{0}\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}}}{\epsilon}\\
&=&\mathcal{O}\left(n\ln(n^{\nu})+n^{3\nu}+\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{\mathcal{R}}_{0}}(n^{1-\nu}+n^{2\nu})}{\epsilon}\right).\end{array}}\vskip-2.15277pt

This expression together with
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{K}\big]=n+\mathbb{E}\big[\hat{\mathcal{T}}_{K}\big]
lead to (
43
).
Clearly, if
\nu=\frac{1}{3}
, then we get
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{K}\big]=\mathcal{O}\big(n\ln(n^{1/3})+\frac{n^{2/3}}{\epsilon}\big)
.

\square

Our next result is the complexity of (
VFOG
) using the SAGA estimator (
SAGA
).

Corollary 3

Suppose that Assumptions
2
-
4
hold for (
GE
) under the finite-sum setting (
2
).
Let
\{(x^{k},y^{k},z^{k})\}
be generated by (
VFOG
) using the SAGA estimator
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
from (
SAGA
) for
Gy^{k}
and the parameters as in Theorem
4.1
.
Suppose that
n\geq\frac{80\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}}
and we choose
b_{k}
in (
SAGA
) as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}b_{k}:=\begin{cases}\big\lfloor\sqrt[3]{\frac{10\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}}}n^{2/3}+\frac{4n}{k+s+1}\big\rfloor&\textrm{if}~k\leq\big\lfloor\frac{4\sqrt[3]{\rho_{c}}}{\sqrt[3]{10\Gamma\eta}}n^{1/3}-s\big\rfloor,\\
\big\lfloor 2\sqrt[3]{\frac{10\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}}}n^{2/3}\big\rfloor&\textrm{otherwise}.\end{cases}\end{array}

(39)

Then, we have
1\leq b_{k}\leq n
, and for any
\epsilon>0
, the expected total number of oracle calls
\mathbf{G}_{\xi}y^{k}
in (
VFOG
) is at most

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{T}_{K}\big]&=&\mathcal{O}\left(n\ln(n^{1/3})+\frac{\mathcal{R}_{0}n^{2/3}}{\epsilon}\right)\end{array}

(40)

to achieve
\mathbb{E}\big[\|Gx^{K}+v^{K}\|^{2}\big]\leq\epsilon^{2}
.

Proof

As indicated by Lemma
2
,
\widetilde{G}y^{k}
generated by (
SAGA
) satisfies Definition
2
with
\kappa_{k}:=\frac{b_{k}}{2n}
and
\Theta_{k}:=\frac{5n}{b_{k}^{2}}
.
For given
\nu\in[0,1/2]
and
c_{1}>0
, let us choose
b_{k}
as

\begin{array}[]{lcl}b_{k}&=&\begin{cases}c_{1}n^{2\nu}+\frac{4n}{k+s+1}&\textrm{if $k\leq\lfloor\frac{4n^{1-2\nu}}{c_{1}}-s\rfloor$},\\
2c_{1}n^{2\nu}&\textrm{otherwise}.\end{cases}\end{array}

(41)

Since
\kappa_{k}:=\frac{b_{k}}{2n}
and
\Theta_{k}:=\frac{5n}{b_{k}^{2}}
, the condition (
31
) holds if
b_{k}\geq\frac{10n^{2}\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}b_{k}^{2}}+\frac{4n}{k+s+1}
.
Because
b_{k}
is updated by (
41
), the last condition is satisfied if
b_{k}\geq\frac{10\Gamma\eta n^{2(1-2\nu)}}{\rho_{c}c_{1}^{2}}+\frac{4n}{k+s+1}
, which automatically holds if we impose
\frac{10n^{2(1-2\nu)}\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}c_{1}^{2}}=c_{1}n^{2\nu}
.
The last equality holds if
\nu=\frac{1}{3}
and
c_{1}:=\sqrt[3]{\frac{10\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}}}
.
Substituting these values into (
41
), we obtain
b_{k}
as in (
39
).
From (
31
), we can observe that
\Gamma\eta>\rho_{c}
, leading to
b\geq 1
.

Since
b_{k}\leq n
, we need to impose
2\sqrt[3]{\frac{10\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}}}n^{2/3}\leq n
, leading to
n\geq\frac{80\Gamma\eta}{\rho_{c}}
.
In addition, for
b_{k}
in (
39
), we can easily check that it satisfies
b_{k-1}-\frac{b_{k}b_{k-1}}{4n}\leq b_{k}\leq b_{k-1}
in Lemma
2
.

Now, for a given tolerance
\epsilon>0
, from (
32
